# Anatomy — Multi-Year PYQ Pool

> **Source:** PrepLadder last-5-year PYQ compilation (student recall reconstructions).  
> **Use:** Personal study notes. ~20 questions spanning multiple years.

---

### Q1 — Anatomical snuff box lateral boundary

Which structures form the **lateral** boundary of the anatomical snuff box?

- A. Extensor pollicis brevis and abductor pollicis longus
- B. Extensor pollicis longus and abductor pollicis brevis
- C. Extensor pollicis longus and extensor pollicis brevis
- D. Abductor pollicis longus and abductor pollicis brevis

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A. Extensor pollicis brevis and abductor pollicis longus** — Lateral (radial) boundary. Medial (ulnar) boundary = extensor pollicis longus. Floor = scaphoid and trapezium; radial artery passes through.
</details>

---

### Q2 — Facial nerve trunk identification

Which landmark is NOT used for identifying the facial nerve trunk?

- A. Retrograde dissection from distal branch
- B. Tragal pointer
- C. Inferior belly of omohyoid muscle
- D. Posterior belly of digastric

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**C. Inferior belly of omohyoid** — Omohyoid is a neck muscle with no relationship to the parotid/facial nerve. Standard landmarks: tragal pointer (1 cm medial and inferior), posterior belly of digastric, tympanomastoid suture, stylomastoid foramen.
</details>

---

### Q3 — Fetal anatomical closure timing

Which statement regarding anatomical closures is **incorrect**?

- A. Foramen ovale closes by 3 to 5 days
- B. Ductus venosus closes by 2 to 3 weeks
- C. Right umbilical vein is absent after birth
- D. Ductus arteriosus closes by 2 to 3 weeks

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A. Foramen ovale closes by 3–5 days** — Functional closure is immediate at birth (pressure reversal), but anatomical closure (fusion) takes weeks to months. Patent foramen ovale persists in ~25% of adults.
</details>

---

### Q4 — Superior cerebellar artery thrombosis

A patient presents with ataxia and incoordination. Which artery is thrombosed?

- A. Posterior cerebral
- B. Middle cerebral
- C. Internal carotid
- D. Superior cerebellar

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**D. Superior cerebellar artery** — SCA occlusion: ipsilateral limb ataxia, Horner syndrome, contralateral pain/temperature loss; also affects nucleus dentatus and spinothalamic tract.
</details>

---

### Q5 — Sternothyroid and thyroid swelling

Which structure prevents upward extension of thyroid swelling by attaching to the thyroid cartilage?

- A. Pretracheal fascia
- B. Sternothyroid
- C. Thyrohyoid membrane
- D. Ligament of Berry

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B. Sternothyroid** — Inserts on the thyroid cartilage oblique line; mechanically tethers the thyroid, preventing cephalad expansion. Hence thyroid swellings move on swallowing but rarely extend superiorly to the thyroid cartilage.
</details>

---

### Q6 — Aneurysm at optic chiasm

An aneurysm most likely to damage the optic chiasma arises from:

- A. Anterior communicating artery
- B. Anterior choroidal artery
- C. Middle cerebral artery
- D. Anterior cerebral artery

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A. Anterior communicating artery** — AComm lies immediately above the optic chiasm; aneurysm rupture/expansion → bitemporal hemianopia. Most common site for saccular aneurysms.
</details>

---

### Q7 — Phrenicocolic ligament and spleen

Which structure prevents vertical/downward spleen descent in massive splenomegaly?

- A. Lienorenal ligament
- B. Gastrosplenic ligament
- C. Phrenicocolic ligament
- D. Lienophrenic ligament

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**C. Phrenicocolic ligament** — Connects left colic flexure to diaphragm; acts as a "hammock" supporting the spleen's lower pole. Explains why the spleen enlarges diagonally toward right iliac fossa.
</details>

---

### Q8 — Circumvallate papillae histology

Identify the papillae described as having a circular groove surrounding them:

- A. Fungiform
- B. Circumvallate
- C. Filiform
- D. Foliate

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B. Circumvallate (vallate) papillae** — Large, 8–12 in number, arranged in a V-shape (sulcus terminalis); surrounded by a deep groove; taste buds in walls of groove; Von Ebner's glands open into the groove for lipase.
</details>

---

### Q9 — Musculocutaneous nerve injury (elbow flexion + supination loss)

A 7-year-old with humerus fracture has elbow flexion difficulty, supination loss, and lateral forearm sensory loss. Which nerve is injured?

- A. Median nerve
- B. Radial nerve
- C. Musculocutaneous nerve
- D. Ulnar nerve

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**C. Musculocutaneous nerve** — Innervates biceps brachii (elbow flexion, supination), brachialis (elbow flexion), and coracobrachialis; terminates as lateral cutaneous nerve of forearm (sensory to lateral forearm).
</details>

---

### Q10 — Lingual nerve in submandibular surgery

During submandibular gland resection, which nerve is most likely damaged?

- A. Nerve to mylohyoid
- B. Inferior alveolar nerve
- C. Lingual nerve
- D. Hypoglossal nerve

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**C. Lingual nerve** — Runs in close proximity to submandibular duct; carries taste and general sensation from anterior 2/3 of tongue; also carries chorda tympani (secretomotor to submandibular gland).
</details>

---

### Q11 — Falciform ligament

The marked peritoneal fold (structure A) gives rise to:

- A. Gastrosplenic ligament
- B. Lienorenal ligament
- C. Falciform ligament
- D. Gastrophrenic ligament

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**C. Falciform ligament** — Connects liver to anterior abdominal wall and diaphragm; contains ligamentum teres (obliterated umbilical vein) in its free edge.
</details>

---

### Q12 — Saphenous nerve injury after varicose vein surgery

Post-varicose vein surgery: sensory loss in medial leg and foot. Which nerve?

- A. Sural nerve
- B. Superficial peroneal nerve
- C. Deep peroneal nerve
- D. Saphenous nerve

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**D. Saphenous nerve** — Terminal branch of femoral nerve; runs alongside great saphenous vein → at risk during vein stripping; supplies medial leg and medial foot.
</details>

---

### Q13 — Islets of Langerhans histology

Identify the structure shown as clustered endocrine cells:

- A. Lymph node
- B. Glomerulus
- C. Spleen
- D. Pancreatic islet cells

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**D. Pancreatic islet cells (Islets of Langerhans)** — α-cells (glucagon), β-cells (insulin, ~70%), δ-cells (somatostatin), PP cells (pancreatic polypeptide). Richly vascularised endocrine clusters in exocrine pancreas.
</details>

---

### Q14 — Fibrocartilage identification

Cartilage with collagen fibres arranged in parallel bundles is:

- A. Non-articular hyaline cartilage
- B. Articular hyaline cartilage
- C. Elastic (yellow) cartilage
- D. White fibrocartilage

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**D. White fibrocartilage** — Contains abundant Type I collagen in parallel bundles; found in intervertebral discs (annulus fibrosus), symphysis pubis, menisci; withstands compression and shear.
</details>

---

### Q15 — Trigeminal nerve division for migraine

Unilateral throbbing headache with photophobia, lacrimation, hemifacial pain on tooth clenching. Which division is involved?

- A. V1 (ophthalmic)
- B. V2 (maxillary)
- C. V3 (mandibular)
- D. All divisions

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A. V1 (ophthalmic division)** — Trigeminal neuralgia most commonly involves V2 and V3; but for migraine with photo/phonophobia and periorbital pain, the ophthalmic division (V1) and trigeminal-vascular system are implicated.
</details>

---

### Q16 — Head rotation joint

Which joint permits rotation of the head (looking left and right)?

- A. Atlanto-occipital
- B. Atlanto-axial (atlanto-axial)
- C. C2–C3
- D. C3–C4

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B. Atlanto-axial joint** — Pivot joint; the dens (odontoid process) of axis acts as a pivot; ~50% of cervical rotation occurs here. Atlanto-occipital = nodding (flexion/extension).
</details>

---

### Q17 — Right coronary artery: false statement

Which statement about the right coronary artery is **false**?

- A. Diameter less than left coronary artery
- B. Arises from right aortic sinus
- C. Gives rise to circumflex coronary branch
- D. Right conal artery is its first branch

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**C. Gives rise to circumflex coronary branch** — The circumflex artery is a branch of the LEFT coronary artery (left main → LAD + circumflex). RCA gives conus, SA nodal, right marginal, and PDA branches.
</details>

---

### Q18 — Omphalocele embryology

Omphalocele formation is based on:

- A. Excessive intestinal length
- B. Physiological hernia failing to return
- C. Liver herniation
- D. Umbilicus herniation

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B. Physiological hernia failing to return** — Normal fetal midgut herniates into umbilical cord at week 6 and returns by week 10. Failure to return = omphalocele (covered by amnion). Gastroschisis = lateral defect, no sac.
</details>

---

### Q19 — Internal capsule fibre type

The fibres at the genu of the internal capsule in a brain scan are:

- A. Short association fibres
- B. Long association fibres
- C. Projection fibres
- D. Commissural fibres

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**C. Projection fibres** — The internal capsule contains projection fibres (corticospinal, corticobulbar, thalamocortical, corticopontine). Commissural fibres pass through corpus callosum; association fibres are ipsilateral.
</details>

---

### Q20 — Calot's triangle boundaries

Which structure does NOT form the boundaries of Calot's triangle?

- A. Common hepatic duct
- B. Cystic duct
- C. Cystic artery
- D. Gallbladder

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**D. Gallbladder** — Calot's triangle (hepatocystic triangle): bounded by cystic duct (below), common hepatic duct (medially), and liver inferior surface (above). The cystic artery runs within it. Gallbladder is not a boundary.
</details>

---

### Q21 — Cerebellum / Purkinje cell inhibition

6-year-old with ataxia, broad-based gait, slow speech post-chickenpox. Loss of inhibitory signals to which structure?

- A. Golgi cell
- B. Basket cell
- C. Vestibular nuclei
- D. Deep cerebellar nuclei

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**D. Deep cerebellar nuclei** — Purkinje cells (GABAergic) tonically inhibit deep cerebellar nuclei (dentate, emboliform, globose, fastigial). Loss of inhibition → cerebellar ataxia. Post-varicella cerebellar ataxia is common and usually self-limiting.

</details>

<!-- SOURCE: prepladder.com high-yield anatomy | OPTIONS_GENERATED: false -->

### Q22 — Neural tube / Rachischisis

1-day-old with spinal cord protruding through open vertebral defect, hairy patch on back — defect cause?

- A. Non-fusion of anterior neuropore
- B. Non-fusion of posterior neuropore
- C. Non-fusion of both neuropores
- D. Failure of caudal cell mass regression

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B. Non-fusion of posterior neuropore** — Posterior neuropore closes by day 27. Failure → rachischisis (open spina bifida). Anterior neuropore closure failure = anencephaly. Spina bifida occulta = bony defect only, no neural tissue exposed.

</details>

<!-- SOURCE: prepladder.com high-yield anatomy | OPTIONS_GENERATED: false -->

### Q23 — Sensory pathways / Stereognosis

Patient identifies an object by touch with eyes closed — pathway tested?

- A. Posterior column–medial lemniscus
- B. Posterior spinocerebellar tract
- C. Spino-olivary tract
- D. Lateral spinothalamic tract

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A. Posterior column–medial lemniscus** — Stereognosis (object recognition by touch alone) requires fine discriminative touch, vibration, proprioception → carried by posterior column → decussates at medulla → VPL thalamus → parietal cortex.

</details>

<!-- SOURCE: prepladder.com high-yield anatomy | OPTIONS_GENERATED: false -->

### Q24 — Inner ear / Endolymphatic sac

The endolymphatic sac is formed by fusion of ductules from which structures?

- A. Cochlea and saccule
- B. Utricle and saccule
- C. Semicircular canals only
- D. Cochlea and semicircular canals

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B. Utricle and saccule** — Utricle and saccule each give off small ductules that fuse to form Y-shaped endolymphatic duct → traverses vestibular aqueduct → terminates as endolymphatic sac in posterior cranial fossa. Menière's disease = endolymphatic hydrops.

</details>

<!-- SOURCE: prepladder.com high-yield anatomy | OPTIONS_GENERATED: false -->

### Q25 — Sacral hiatus / Structures transmitted

Structures passing through the sacral hiatus?

- A. S1–S4 nerve roots + filum terminale
- B. S5 sacral nerves + coccygeal nerves + filum terminale externum
- C. S3–S5 + cauda equina
- D. Sacral arteries + S4–S5 nerves

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B. S5 sacral nerves + coccygeal nerves + filum terminale externum** — Sacral hiatus (deficient posterior arch of S5) transmits: pair of 5th sacral nerves, coccygeal nerves (Co1), and filum terminale externum. Site for caudal epidural block.

</details>

<!-- SOURCE: prepladder.com high-yield anatomy | OPTIONS_GENERATED: false -->

### Q26 — Gynaecological anatomy / Culdocentesis

For culdocentesis, through which structure is the needle inserted?

- A. Anterior vaginal fornix
- B. Posterior vaginal fornix
- C. Anterior wall of rectum
- D. Posterior wall of uterus

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B. Posterior vaginal fornix** — Culdocentesis accesses the pouch of Douglas (rectouterine pouch) through the posterior fornix to aspirate fluid (blood in ectopic pregnancy, pus in PID). Also called posterior colpotomy.

</details>

<!-- SOURCE: prepladder.com high-yield anatomy | OPTIONS_GENERATED: false -->

### Q27 — Neonatal anatomy / Hirschsprung disease

4-day-old baby, no defecation, normal feeding, large fecal mass expelled after rectal exam — diagnosis?

- A. Imperforate anus
- B. Anal agenesis
- C. Anorectal agenesis
- D. Colonic aganglionosis (Hirschsprung disease)

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**D. Colonic aganglionosis** — Hirschsprung disease: failure of neural crest cell migration → absent Auerbach/Meissner plexus in rectosigmoid → functional obstruction → megacolon proximal to aganglionic segment. Rectal biopsy: absent ganglion cells + hypertrophied nerve trunks.

</details>

<!-- SOURCE: prepladder.com high-yield anatomy | OPTIONS_GENERATED: false -->

### Q28 — Hip flexors / Strongest

Strongest hip flexor muscle?

- A. Sartorius
- B. Gluteus maximus
- C. Iliopsoas
- D. Pectineus

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**C. Iliopsoas** — Iliacus (L2-L4) + psoas major (L1-L3) → strongest hip flexor. Sartorius and rectus femoris are accessory. Tested with straight leg raise. Psoas abscess → pain on passive hip extension (psoas sign).

</details>

<!-- SOURCE: prepladder.com high-yield anatomy | OPTIONS_GENERATED: false -->

### Q29 — Coronary anatomy / NOT branch of RCA

Which vessel is NOT a branch of the right coronary artery?

- A. Anterior interventricular artery (LAD)
- B. SA nodal artery (55% cases)
- C. Posterior interventricular artery (PDA)
- D. AV nodal artery

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A. Anterior interventricular artery (LAD)** — LAD is the largest branch of the LEFT anterior descending artery. RCA branches: SA nodal (55%), RV marginals, posterior interventricular (PDA in right-dominant heart — 85%), AV nodal.

</details>

<!-- SOURCE: prepladder.com high-yield anatomy | OPTIONS_GENERATED: false -->

### Q30 — Nerve injury / Surgical neck of humerus

Fracture of surgical neck of humerus — most commonly injured nerve?

- A. Musculocutaneous nerve
- B. Median nerve
- C. Ulnar nerve
- D. Axillary nerve

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**D. Axillary nerve** — Axillary nerve (C5, C6) winds around surgical neck of humerus in quadrangular space; injury → loss of deltoid (shoulder abduction 15–90°) + teres minor + sensory loss over regimental badge area (lateral arm).

</details>

<!-- SOURCE: prepladder.com high-yield anatomy | OPTIONS_GENERATED: false -->

### Q31 — Nerve of 2nd pharyngeal arch

The nerve of the second pharyngeal arch is:

- A. Glossopharyngeal nerve
- B. Trigeminal nerve
- C. Facial nerve
- D. Vagal nerve

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**C. Facial nerve** — Arch nerves: 1st = mandibular (V3); 2nd = facial (VII); 3rd = glossopharyngeal (IX); 4th = superior laryngeal (X); 6th = recurrent laryngeal (X). 2nd arch muscles (stapedius, stylohyoid, posterior digastric, muscles of facial expression) all supplied by facial nerve.

</details>

<!-- SOURCE: Anatomy_NEET_PG_PYT20182023_1_1.pdf 2018 | OPTIONS_GENERATED: false -->

### Q32 — Aortic hiatus — structure NOT transmitted

The structure NOT passing through the aortic opening (T12) of the diaphragm is:

- A. Aorta
- B. Azygous vein
- C. Thoracic duct
- D. Vagal trunk

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**D. Vagal trunk** — Aortic hiatus (T12): aorta, thoracic duct, azygos vein, hemiazygos vein. Oesophageal hiatus (T10): oesophagus, right and left vagal trunks, left gastric vessels. Caval hiatus (T8): IVC, right phrenic nerve.

</details>

<!-- SOURCE: Anatomy_NEET_PG_PYT20182023_1_1.pdf 2018 | OPTIONS_GENERATED: false -->

### Q33 — SVA column — NOT a member

Which cranial nerve nucleus is NOT part of the special visceral afferent (SVA) column?

- A. Facial nerve
- B. Trochlear nerve
- C. Vagus nerve
- D. Glossopharyngeal nerve

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B. Trochlear nerve** — Trochlear nucleus = GSE column (innervates superior oblique). SVA column carries taste: facial (VII — anterior 2/3 tongue), glossopharyngeal (IX — posterior 1/3), vagus (X — epiglottis/pharynx). Remember: trochlear is purely motor.

</details>

<!-- SOURCE: Anatomy_NEET_PG_PYT20182023_1_1.pdf 2018 | OPTIONS_GENERATED: false -->

### Q34 — Sibson's fascia — incorrect statement

Which is INCORRECT regarding Sibson's fascia?

- A. Attached to inner border of 2nd rib
- B. Covers apical part of the lung
- C. Strengthened by scalenus muscle fibers
- D. Subclavian vessels pass above it

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A. Attached to inner border of 2nd rib** — Sibson's fascia (suprapleural membrane) attaches to inner border of **1st** rib (not 2nd) and transverse process of C7. Covers lung apex. Reinforced by scalenus minimus. Subclavian vessels lie on its superior surface.

</details>

<!-- SOURCE: Anatomy_NEET_PG_PYT20182023_1_1.pdf 2018 | OPTIONS_GENERATED: false -->

### Q35 — Broca's area location

Broca's area (motor speech area) is situated in the:

- A. Inferior frontal gyrus
- B. Superior temporal gyrus
- C. Angular gyrus
- D. Postcentral gyrus

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A. Inferior frontal gyrus** — Broca's area (Brodmann 44, 45): pars opercularis + pars triangularis of dominant inferior frontal gyrus. Wernicke's = superior temporal gyrus (22). Angular gyrus (39) = reading. Broca's aphasia = non-fluent, comprehension intact.

</details>

<!-- SOURCE: Anatomy_NEET_PG_PYT20182023_1_1.pdf 2018 | OPTIONS_GENERATED: false -->

### Q36 — Interchondral rib joint type

The joint between the 8th and 9th rib attached to the 7th rib is a:

- A. Fibrocartilaginous joint
- B. Symphyseal joint
- C. Synovial joint
- D. Fibrous joint

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**C. Synovial joint** — Interchondral joints (7th–10th) = synovial; allow thoracic expansion during inspiration. Exception: 9th–10th interchondral sometimes absent. Costochondral = synchondrosis; manubriosternal/xiphisternal = symphysis.

</details>

<!-- SOURCE: Anatomy_NEET_PG_PYT20182023_1_1.pdf 2018 | OPTIONS_GENERATED: false -->

### Q37 — Ureteric bud origin

Ureteric bud develops from:

- A. Mesonephric (Wolffian) duct
- B. Metanephros
- C. Pronephros
- D. Urogenital sinus

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A. Mesonephric duct** — Ureteric bud sprouts from mesonephric duct at week 5 → invades metanephric mesoderm → forms ureter, pelvis, calyces, collecting ducts. Failure = renal agenesis. Other mesonephric derivatives: epididymis, vas deferens, seminal vesicle.

</details>

<!-- SOURCE: Anatomy_NEET_PG_PYT20182023_1_1.pdf 2018 | OPTIONS_GENERATED: false -->

### Q38 — Heart development — beating onset

The heart starts to beat in which week?

- A. 2nd week
- B. 4th week
- C. 6th week
- D. 10th week

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B. 4th week** — Cardiac tube beats from day 22–23 (4th week). First functional organ. Septation complete by week 7–8. Heart looping rightward = dextrocardia if reversed.

</details>

<!-- SOURCE: Anatomy_NEET_PG_PYT20182023_1_1.pdf 2018 | OPTIONS_GENERATED: false -->

### Q39 — Tensor of vocal cords

Which muscle is the tensor of vocal cords?

- A. Cricothyroid
- B. Lateral cricoarytenoid
- C. Thyroarytenoid
- D. Posterior cricoarytenoid

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A. Cricothyroid** — Functional roles: Cricothyroid = tensor; Thyroarytenoid = relaxer; PCA = only abductor (safety muscle); LCA = adductor + whispering; Vocalis = modulator. All supplied by RLN except cricothyroid (SLN external branch).

</details>

<!-- SOURCE: Anatomy_NEET_PG_PYT20182023_1_1.pdf 2018 | OPTIONS_GENERATED: false -->

### Q40 — Iliopubic tract — true statement

Which statement is TRUE about the iliopubic tract?

- A. It is a thickening of the lumbar fascia
- B. It lies anterior to the inguinal ligament
- C. Inserts into pubic tubercle in front of conjoint tendon
- D. Its fibers fan out with fascia iliaca laterally

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**D. Its fibers fan out with fascia iliaca laterally** — Iliopubic tract = thickening of transversalis fascia, posterior to inguinal ligament. Medially inserts into pubic tubercle + pectineal line behind conjoint tendon. Laterally blends with fascia iliaca. Key landmark in laparoscopic hernia repair (TEP/TAPP).

</details>

<!-- SOURCE: Anatomy_NEET_PG_PYT20182023_1_1.pdf 2018 | OPTIONS_GENERATED: false -->

### Q41 — Last fontanelle to close

Which fontanelle closes last?

- A. Anterior
- B. Posterior
- C. Sphenoidal
- D. Mastoid

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A. Anterior** — Closure timeline: Posterior + Sphenoidal = 2–3 months; Mastoid = 12 months; Anterior = 18–24 months (latest). Delayed closure: hypothyroidism, rickets, hydrocephalus, Down syndrome, achondroplasia.

</details>

<!-- SOURCE: Anatomy_NEET_PG_PYT20182023_1_1.pdf 2018 | OPTIONS_GENERATED: false -->

### Q42 — Hard palate histology

Hard palate contains:

- A. Keratinised epithelium, submucosa, minor salivary glands
- B. Keratinised epithelium, submucosa, no salivary glands
- C. Non-keratinised epithelium, submucosa, minor salivary glands
- D. Non-keratinised epithelium, absent submucosa, minor salivary glands

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A. Keratinised epithelium, submucosa, minor salivary glands** — Hard palate: ortho/parakeratinised stratified squamous epithelium + submucosa + minor mucous salivary glands. Gingiva also keratinised. Soft palate = non-keratinised. Pharynx = non-keratinised stratified squamous.

</details>

<!-- SOURCE: Anatomy_NEET_PG_PYT20182023_1_1.pdf 2018 | OPTIONS_GENERATED: false -->

### Q43 — Prostate lobe — uvula vesicae

Which lobe of the prostate forms the uvula vesicae?

- A. Anterior lobe
- B. Posterior lobe
- C. Median lobe
- D. Lateral lobe

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**C. Median lobe** — Median lobe projects superiorly into bladder forming uvula vesicae behind internal urethral orifice. BPH predominantly affects median + lateral lobes → outflow obstruction. Posterior lobe = zone of carcinoma (PSA elevation).

</details>

<!-- SOURCE: Anatomy_NEET_PG_PYT20182023_1_1.pdf 2018 | OPTIONS_GENERATED: false -->

### Q44 — 1st pharyngeal arch — NOT a derivative

1st pharyngeal arch gives rise to all EXCEPT:

- A. Medial pterygoid
- B. Levator veli palatini
- C. Lateral pterygoid
- D. Tensor veli palatini

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B. Levator veli palatini** — Levator veli palatini = 4th arch (vagal plexus). 1st arch (V3): temporalis, masseter, lateral + medial pterygoids, mylohyoid, anterior belly digastric, tensor tympani, tensor veli palatini. Mnemonic: "My Muscle Tensor Mates Are Arch 1 Volunteers."

</details>

<!-- SOURCE: Anatomy_NEET_PG_PYT20182023_1_1.pdf 2018 | OPTIONS_GENERATED: false -->

### Q45 — Atlanto-axial joint — head rotation

Movements at which joint permit looking left and right?

- A. Atlanto-occipital
- B. Atlanto-axial
- C. C2–C3
- D. C3–C4

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B. Atlanto-axial** — Atlanto-axial joint = rotation (≈40°) = "No" movement. Three synovial joints: 1 median (dens pivot) + 2 lateral. Atlanto-occipital = "Yes" movement (flexion/extension/nodding). Dens held by transverse atlantal ligament.

</details>

<!-- SOURCE: Anatomy_NEET_PG_PYT20182023_1_1.pdf 2019 | OPTIONS_GENERATED: false -->

### Q46 — Right coronary artery — false statement

All of the following are true about RCA EXCEPT:

- A. Diameter less than left coronary artery
- B. Arises from anterior aortic sinus
- C. Gives rise to circumflex artery
- D. Right conal artery is a branch

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**C. Gives rise to circumflex artery** — Circumflex = branch of **left** coronary artery. RCA branches: SA nodal (55%), right conal, RV marginals, PDA (85% right-dominant hearts), AV nodal. LCA branches: LAD (anterior interventricular) + circumflex.

</details>

<!-- SOURCE: Anatomy_NEET_PG_PYT20182023_1_1.pdf 2019 | OPTIONS_GENERATED: false -->

### Q47 — Omphalocele — pathogenesis

The basis for formation of omphalocele is:

- A. Excessive length of intestine
- B. Physiological hernia failing to reduce
- C. Herniation of liver through wall defect
- D. Herniation of umbilical cord contents

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B. Physiological hernia failing to reduce** — Midgut physiologically herniates into umbilical cord at week 6 (too large for cavity); normally returns by week 10–12. Non-return → omphalocele (covered sac, central defect ≥4 cm, may contain liver + bowel, 50% associated anomalies).

</details>

<!-- SOURCE: Anatomy_NEET_PG_PYT20182023_1_1.pdf 2019 | OPTIONS_GENERATED: false -->

### Q48 — Cantlie line — liver division

Cholecysto-vena caval line (Cantlie line) separates:

- A. Gallbladder and IVC
- B. Porta hepatis and IVC
- C. Caudate lobe and quadrangular lobe
- D. Right and left hemilivers (anatomical lobes)

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**D. Right and left hemilivers** — Cantlie's line: midpoint of gallbladder fossa to midpoint of IVC; contains middle hepatic vein. Basis of Couinaud segmental anatomy (8 segments). True anatomical division regardless of surface markings. Used for hepatic resection planning.

</details>

<!-- SOURCE: Anatomy_NEET_PG_PYT20182023_1_1.pdf 2019 | OPTIONS_GENERATED: false -->

### Q49 — External oblique aponeurosis — NOT a derivative

All derived from external oblique aponeurosis EXCEPT:

- A. Pectineal (Cooper's) ligament
- B. Inguinal (Poupart's) ligament
- C. Lacunar (Gimbernat's) ligament
- D. Linea semilunaris

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**D. Linea semilunaris** — Linea semilunaris = lateral border of rectus sheath from aponeurosis of **internal oblique** (splits to invest rectus). External oblique gives: inguinal ligament, lacunar ligament, pectineal ligament, superficial inguinal ring, reflected inguinal ligament.

</details>

<!-- SOURCE: Anatomy_NEET_PG_PYT20182023_1_1.pdf 2019 | OPTIONS_GENERATED: false -->

### Q50 — Mandibular foramen — nerve

Which nerve passes through the mandibular foramen?

- A. Lingual nerve
- B. Mandibular nerve
- C. Chorda tympani nerve
- D. Inferior alveolar nerve

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**D. Inferior alveolar nerve** — IAN (branch of V3) enters mandibular foramen → mandibular canal → exits as mental nerve at mental foramen (supplies lower lip, chin). Lingual nerve lies anterior to IAN but doesn't enter foramen. IAN gives nerve to mylohyoid before entering foramen.

</details>

<!-- SOURCE: Anatomy_NEET_PG_PYT20182023_1_1.pdf 2019 | OPTIONS_GENERATED: false -->

### Q51 — Calot's triangle — boundary EXCEPT

Calot's triangle boundaries include all EXCEPT:

- A. Common hepatic duct
- B. Cystic duct
- C. Cystic artery
- D. Gall bladder

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**D. Gall bladder** — Calot's original triangle: medially = common hepatic duct; inferiorly = cystic duct; superiorly = cystic artery. Contents: cystic artery branching + Lund's lymph node. Gall bladder is NOT a boundary. Modern hepatobiliary triangle replaces cystic artery with inferior liver surface.

</details>

<!-- SOURCE: Anatomy_NEET_PG_PYT20182023_1_1.pdf 2019 | OPTIONS_GENERATED: false -->

### Q52 — Skin — incorrect statement

Which is INCORRECT regarding skin?

- A. Dermis is derived from ectoderm
- B. Skin constitutes 15% of body weight
- C. Epidermis is derived from ectoderm
- D. Dermis contains type I and III collagen

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A. Dermis is derived from ectoderm** — Dermis = mesoderm (lateral plate mesoderm + somite-derived dermomyotome). Epidermis = ectoderm. Dermis: type I (90%) + type III collagen, elastic fibers, fibroblasts. Skin = largest organ (~15% body weight, ~1.7 m² surface area).

</details>

<!-- SOURCE: Anatomy_NEET_PG_PYT20182023_1_1.pdf 2019 | OPTIONS_GENERATED: false -->

### Q53 — De Quervain's tenosynovitis — true statement

Which is TRUE about De Quervain's tenosynovitis?

- A. Fingers are held in mild extension
- B. Affects APL and EPB (1st dorsal compartment)
- C. Most common involvement is index finger
- D. Treatment is always surgical

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B. Affects APL and EPB** — De Quervain's = stenosing tenosynovitis of 1st dorsal compartment (APL + EPB). Most commonly affects **thumb** (not index). Finkelstein's test: ulnar deviation of wrist with thumb in fist → pain over radial styloid. Most respond to conservative therapy; surgery (compartment release) for refractory.

</details>

<!-- SOURCE: Anatomy_NEET_PG_PYT20182023_1_1.pdf 2019 | OPTIONS_GENERATED: false -->

### Q54 — Foot drop — nerve

Foot drop is caused by damage to:

- A. Common peroneal nerve
- B. Femoral nerve
- C. Tibial nerve
- D. Sciatic nerve

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A. Common peroneal nerve** — Common peroneal nerve winds around fibular neck (most vulnerable site) → foot drop = loss of dorsiflexion (tibialis anterior, EHL, EDL) + eversion. Sensory: dorsum of foot. High sciatic injury → also foot drop but with additional tibial nerve deficits.

</details>

<!-- SOURCE: Anatomy_NEET_PG_PYT20182023_1_1.pdf 2019 | OPTIONS_GENERATED: false -->

### Q55 — Anatomical snuffbox — lateral boundary

Which of the following forms the lateral boundary of the anatomical snuffbox?

- A. Extensor pollicis brevis and abductor pollicis longus
- B. Extensor pollicis longus and abductor pollicis brevis
- C. Extensor pollicis longus and extensor pollicis brevis
- D. Abductor pollicis longus and abductor pollicis brevis

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A. EPB and APL** — Lateral = APL + EPB; Medial = EPL; Floor = radial styloid + scaphoid + trapezium + 1st metacarpal; Roof = cephalic vein + superficial radial nerve; Content = radial artery. Scaphoid fracture → tenderness in snuffbox.

</details>

<!-- SOURCE: Anatomy_NEET_PG_PYT20182023_1_1.pdf 2020 | OPTIONS_GENERATED: false -->

### Q56 — Purkinje cell — characteristics

Cerebellar Purkinje cells are:

- A. Excitatory, in granular layer
- B. Inhibitory (GABAergic), in single Purkinje cell layer
- C. Excitatory, in molecular layer
- D. Inhibitory, scattered throughout all layers

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B. Inhibitory (GABAergic), in single Purkinje cell layer** — Purkinje cells: largest neurons in CNS; flask-shaped; single layer between molecular + granular; receive from climbing fibers (inferior olive) + parallel fibers; project inhibitory axons to deep cerebellar nuclei; sole output of cerebellar cortex.

</details>

<!-- SOURCE: Anatomy_NEET_PG_PYT20182023_1_1.pdf 2020 | OPTIONS_GENERATED: false -->

### Q57 — Facial nerve trunk — NOT a landmark

Which is NOT used for identification of the facial nerve trunk during parotidectomy?

- A. Retrograde dissection from distal branch
- B. Tragal pointer (Conley's pointer)
- C. Inferior belly of omohyoid muscle
- D. Posterior belly of digastric

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**C. Inferior belly of omohyoid** — Facial nerve trunk landmarks: Tragal pointer (1 cm deep + inferior to tip of tragal cartilage), posterior belly of digastric (nerve superior to its upper border), squamotympanic fissure, styloid process, mastoid drilling. Omohyoid lies in the neck below the parotid region — not a facial nerve landmark.

</details>

<!-- SOURCE: Anatomy_NEET_PG_PYT20182023_1_1.pdf 2020 | OPTIONS_GENERATED: false -->

### Q58 — Foramen ovale — closure timing

Which is a FALSE statement about fetal circulatory adjustments?

- A. Anatomical closure of foramen ovale occurs within 3–5 days
- B. Functional closure of ductus arteriosus occurs within 10–15 hours
- C. Left umbilical vein persists as ligamentum teres
- D. Anatomical closure of ductus arteriosus occurs by 2–3 weeks

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A. Anatomical closure of foramen ovale within 3–5 days** — Foramen ovale: functional closure at birth (LA pressure > RA); anatomical closure takes **months to years** (PFO present in 25% adults). DA: functional = 10–15 h; anatomical = 10–21 days. Ductus venosus: 2–3 weeks anatomical closure.

</details>

<!-- SOURCE: Anatomy_NEET_PG_PYT20182023_1_1.pdf 2020 | OPTIONS_GENERATED: false -->

### Q59 — Uncinate process — vessel at risk

Tumor of uncinate process of pancreas affects which vessel?

- A. Superior mesenteric artery
- B. Portal vein
- C. Common hepatic artery
- D. Inferior mesenteric artery

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A. Superior mesenteric artery** — SMA arises from aorta, passes posterior then anterior to uncinate process (hooks around it). SMA involvement = unresectable pancreatic cancer. Neck of pancreas: portal vein + SMV posterior. Head: common bile duct embedded within.

</details>

<!-- SOURCE: Anatomy_NEET_PG_PYT20182023_1_1.pdf 2020 | OPTIONS_GENERATED: false -->

### Q60 — Cerebellar ataxia — artery

Ataxia and incoordination = thrombosis of which artery?

- A. Posterior cerebral
- B. Middle cerebral
- C. Internal carotid
- D. Superior cerebellar

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**D. Superior cerebellar artery** — SCA thrombosis → cerebellar hemispheric infarct: ipsilateral ataxia, dysmetria, nystagmus. SCA = superior surface of cerebellum. PICA → lateral medullary syndrome (Wallenberg). AICA → lateral pontine syndrome. MCA → motor/sensory cortex.

</details>

<!-- SOURCE: Anatomy_NEET_PG_PYT20182023_1_1.pdf 2020 | OPTIONS_GENERATED: false -->
### Q61 — Claw hand — intrinsic muscles

Extension of MCP joint + flexion of IP joints — muscle group affected?

- A. Extensor digitorum
- B. Interossei and lumbricals
- C. Abductor pollicis longus
- D. Adductor pollicis

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B. Interossei and lumbricals** — Normal function: flex MCP + extend IP. Loss (ulnar palsy) → MCP hyperextension (extensor digitorum unopposed) + IP flexion (FDP/FDS unopposed) = claw hand. Ulnar claw (ring + little most pronounced). LOAF muscles via median nerve unaffected.

</details>

<!-- SOURCE: Anatomy_NEET_PG_PYT20182023_1_1.pdf 2020 | OPTIONS_GENERATED: false -->

### Q62 — Meiosis — location

Meiosis occurs in:

- A. Adrenal gland
- B. Adult ovary
- C. Prepubertal testis
- D. Hypothalamus

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B. Adult ovary** — Meiosis in germ cells only. Oogonia enter meiosis I in fetal ovary → arrest at diplotene prophase I → resume at each ovulation cycle. Males: meiosis begins at puberty in primary spermatocytes. Adrenal, hypothalamus = somatic cells → mitosis only.

</details>

<!-- SOURCE: Anatomy_NEET_PG_PYT20182023_1_1.pdf 2020 | OPTIONS_GENERATED: false -->

### Q63 — Long left recurrent laryngeal nerve — arch origin

Long left recurrent laryngeal nerve is due to persistence of which aortic arch?

- A. 3rd arch
- B. 4th arch
- C. 5th arch
- D. 6th arch

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**D. 6th arch** — Left 6th arch → ductus arteriosus (→ ligamentum arteriosum). RLN initially hooked below 6th arch; left hook under ligamentum arteriosum = longer course. Right: 4th arch → right subclavian → right RLN hooks below subclavian.

</details>

<!-- SOURCE: Anatomy_NEET_PG_PYT20182023_1_1.pdf 2020 | OPTIONS_GENERATED: false -->

### Q64 — Lateral pontine syndrome — lesion site

Right-sided facial weakness + left leg pain/temperature loss = lesion at?

- A. Medial medulla
- B. Lateral pons
- C. Medial pons
- D. Lateral medulla

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B. Lateral pons** — AICA territory: ipsilateral VII (facial) + ipsilateral VI (may) + contralateral body pain/temperature (spinothalamic). Wallenberg (PICA, lateral medulla): ipsilateral face (V descending nucleus) + contralateral body pain/temp. Weber (posterior cerebral, midbrain): CN III palsy + contralateral hemiplegia.

</details>

<!-- SOURCE: Anatomy_NEET_PG_PYT20182023_1_1.pdf 2020 | OPTIONS_GENERATED: false -->

### Q65 — Anal canal lymph drainage

Enlarged inguinal lymph node — most likely primary source?

- A. Testicular cancer
- B. Anal cancer (below dentate line)
- C. Sigmoid colon cancer
- D. Prostate cancer

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B. Anal cancer (below dentate line)** — Below dentate line → superficial inguinal nodes. Above dentate → internal iliac. Testes → para-aortic (L1). Prostate, sigmoid, rectum → internal/common iliac. Scrotal skin → inguinal. Key: level of dentate line determines lymphatic drainage direction.

</details>

<!-- SOURCE: Anatomy_NEET_PG_PYT20182023_1_1.pdf 2020 | OPTIONS_GENERATED: false -->

### Q66 — Elastic cartilage — identification

Histological section with elastic fibers in matrix, chondrocytes in lacunae, perichondrium present, no calcification. Type of cartilage?

- A. Hyaline cartilage
- B. Elastic cartilage
- C. Articular hyaline cartilage
- D. Fibrocartilage

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B. Elastic cartilage** — Elastic cartilage: type II collagen + elastic fibers, has perichondrium, does NOT calcify. Sites: epiglottis, pinna, EAM, Eustachian tube, apices of arytenoids. Fibrocartilage: type I + II, NO perichondrium. Hyaline: type II, no elastic fibers, calcifies with age.

</details>

<!-- SOURCE: Anatomy_NEET_PG_PYT20182023_1_1.pdf 2020 | OPTIONS_GENERATED: false -->

### Q67 — Optic chiasma aneurysm — vessel

An aneurysm causing damage to the optic chiasma is most likely from which artery?

- A. Anterior communicating artery
- B. Anterior choroidal artery
- C. Middle cerebral artery
- D. Anterior cerebral artery

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A. Anterior communicating artery** — AComA lies directly above optic chiasma → aneurysm → bitemporal hemianopia (compression of crossing nasal fibers). Most common site of berry aneurysm = AComA (30–35%). Pituitary tumors compress chiasma from below.

</details>

<!-- SOURCE: Anatomy_NEET_PG_PYT20182023_1_1.pdf 2021 | OPTIONS_GENERATED: false -->

### Q68 — Phrenicocolic ligament — spleen

Which structure prevents vertical and downward descent of the spleen?

- A. Lienorenal ligament
- B. Gastrosplenic ligament
- C. Phrenicocolic ligament
- D. Lienophrenic ligament

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**C. Phrenicocolic ligament** — Phrenicocolic ligament: splenic flexure → diaphragm; acts as "shelf" below spleen (not attached to spleen). Lienorenal: spleen hilum → left kidney (contains splenic vessels + tail of pancreas). Gastrosplenic: greater curve of stomach → spleen (short gastric + left gastroepiploic).

</details>

<!-- SOURCE: Anatomy_NEET_PG_PYT20182023_1_1.pdf 2021 | OPTIONS_GENERATED: false -->

### Q69 — Circumvallate papillae — histology

Image shows large cylindrical papillae (8–12), non-keratinised stratified squamous epithelium, taste buds on lateral walls with surrounding groove. Papillae type?

- A. Fungiform
- B. Filiform
- C. Vallate (circumvallate)
- D. Foliate

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**C. Vallate (circumvallate)** — Circumvallate papillae: largest (1–3 mm diameter), 8–12 in number, arranged in V (sulcus terminalis); Von Ebner's serous glands drain into groove; taste buds on lateral walls (not dorsal surface). Filiform = most numerous, no taste buds. Fungiform = mushroom-shaped, anterior 2/3, taste buds on top.

</details>

<!-- SOURCE: Anatomy_NEET_PG_PYT20182023_1_1.pdf 2021 | OPTIONS_GENERATED: false -->

### Q70 — Musculocutaneous nerve injury

Humerus fracture: difficulty in elbow flexion + supination + sensory loss over lateral forearm. Nerve injured?

- A. Median nerve
- B. Radial nerve
- C. Musculocutaneous nerve
- D. Ulnar nerve

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**C. Musculocutaneous nerve** — Musculocutaneous nerve (C5, C6, C7): motor = biceps (flexion + supination), brachialis, coracobrachialis; sensory = lateral cutaneous nerve of forearm (lateral forearm + lateral forearm to wrist). Injured in shoulder dislocation or proximal humerus fractures.

</details>

<!-- SOURCE: Anatomy_NEET_PG_PYT20182023_1_1.pdf 2021 | OPTIONS_GENERATED: false -->

### Q71 — Lingual nerve — submandibular gland surgery

Most likely nerve damaged during submandibular gland resection?

- A. Nerve to mylohyoid
- B. Inferior alveolar nerve
- C. Lingual nerve
- D. Otic nerve

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**C. Lingual nerve** — Lingual nerve winds around Wharton's duct (below and lateral then above and medial) — "Lingual takes a curve around Wharton." Damage → ipsilateral loss of taste + general sensation of anterior 2/3 tongue + loss of secretomotor to sublingual gland.

</details>

<!-- SOURCE: Anatomy_NEET_PG_PYT20182023_1_1.pdf 2021 | OPTIONS_GENERATED: false -->

### Q72 — Saphenous nerve — bypass graft complication

Patient with paresthesia + sensory loss over medial leg and foot following GSV harvest for CABG. Nerve injured?

- A. Superficial peroneal nerve
- B. Saphenous nerve
- C. Femoral nerve
- D. Sural nerve

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B. Saphenous nerve** — Saphenous nerve (terminal branch of femoral nerve, L3–L4) accompanies great saphenous vein → injured during GSV harvest. Supplies: medial leg + medial foot + medial aspect of great toe. Sural nerve (lateral foot) accompanies small saphenous vein.

</details>

<!-- SOURCE: Anatomy_NEET_PG_PYT20182023_1_1.pdf 2021 | OPTIONS_GENERATED: false -->

### Q73 — Uvula sensation — cranial nerve

Which cranial nerve is tested by touching the uvula?

- A. IX — Glossopharyngeal
- B. X — Vagus
- C. XI — Accessory
- D. XII — Hypoglossal

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A. IX — Glossopharyngeal** — CN IX sensory: uvula, soft palate, fauces, tonsils, pharynx, posterior 1/3 tongue, tympanic cavity, Eustachian tube. Motor to uvula = CN X (via pharyngeal plexus). Gag reflex: afferent = IX, efferent = X. Uvula deviation away from lesion = X palsy (not IX).

</details>

<!-- SOURCE: Anatomy_NEET_PG_PYT20182023_1_1.pdf 2021 | OPTIONS_GENERATED: false -->

### Q74 — Ventral mesentery derivative

Structure marked (ventral mesentery) gives rise to which of the following?

- A. Gastrosplenic ligament
- B. Lienorenal ligament
- C. Falciform ligament
- D. Gastrophrenic ligament

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**C. Falciform ligament** — Ventral mesentery derivatives (foregut only): falciform ligament, lesser omentum, right + left triangular ligaments, coronary ligament. Dorsal mesentery: greater omentum, gastrosplenic, lienorenal, mesentery proper, mesocolon, mesoappendix.

</details>

<!-- SOURCE: Anatomy_NEET_PG_PYT20182023_1_1.pdf 2022 | OPTIONS_GENERATED: false -->

### Q75 — Varicose vein surgery — nerve injury

Sensory loss over medial leg and foot after varicose vein surgery — nerve injured?

- A. Sural nerve
- B. Superficial peroneal nerve
- C. Deep peroneal nerve
- D. Saphenous nerve

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**D. Saphenous nerve** — Saphenous nerve accompanies great saphenous vein → injured in varicose vein surgery affecting GSV. Sural = lateral foot (small saphenous). Superficial peroneal = dorsum of foot. Deep peroneal = 1st web space. Medial leg + foot = saphenous territory.

</details>

<!-- SOURCE: Anatomy_NEET_PG_PYT20182023_1_1.pdf 2022 | OPTIONS_GENERATED: false -->

### Q76 — Fibrocartilage — histology

Image shows collagen fibers in bundles, rows of chondrocytes, no perichondrium. Type of cartilage?

- A. Hyaline cartilage
- B. Articular hyaline cartilage
- C. Yellow (elastic) cartilage
- D. White fibrocartilage

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**D. White fibrocartilage** — Fibrocartilage: type I + II collagen, thick bundles, NO perichondrium, calcifies only during bone repair. Sites: intervertebral discs, menisci, pubic symphysis, TMJ disc, sternoclavicular disc, glenoid/acetabular labrum, tendons lining bony grooves.

</details>

<!-- SOURCE: Anatomy_NEET_PG_PYT20182023_1_1.pdf 2022 | OPTIONS_GENERATED: false -->

### Q77 — Pancreatic islet cells — histology

Histological image shows clusters of pale-staining polygonal cells surrounded by acinar pancreatic tissue. Structure?

- A. Hassall's corpuscles
- B. Glomerulus
- C. Spleen white pulp
- D. Pancreatic islet cells

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**D. Pancreatic islet cells** — Islets of Langerhans: endocrine pancreas, 1–2 million islets, richly vascularized. Cell types: β (insulin, 70%), α (glucagon, 20%), δ (somatostatin, 10%), PP cells. More numerous in tail. Type 1 DM = β cell destruction (autoimmune); Type 2 = β cell dysfunction + resistance.

</details>

<!-- SOURCE: Anatomy_NEET_PG_PYT20182023_1_1.pdf 2022 | OPTIONS_GENERATED: false -->

### Q78 — Deep inguinal ring — structure forming it

A defect in which of the following forms the deep inguinal ring?

- A. Internal oblique muscle
- B. External oblique muscle
- C. Parietal peritoneum
- D. Fascia transversalis

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**D. Fascia transversalis** — Deep inguinal ring = oval defect in fascia transversalis, 1.25 cm above midpoint of inguinal ligament, lateral to inferior epigastric artery. Indirect hernia passes through deep ring. Superficial inguinal ring = defect in external oblique aponeurosis. Hasselbach's triangle: epigastric artery (lateral), inguinal ligament (inferior), rectus sheath (medial).

</details>

<!-- SOURCE: Anatomy_NEET_PG_PYT20182023_1_1.pdf 2023 | OPTIONS_GENERATED: false -->

### Q79 — Lateral pterygoid — TMJ attachment

Patient with jaw locking while yawning. Which muscle is attached to the articular disc of the TMJ?

- A. Lateral pterygoid
- B. Temporalis
- C. Medial pterygoid
- D. Masseter

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A. Lateral pterygoid** — Lateral pterygoid: upper head → TMJ disc + capsule; lower head → condyle neck. Pulls disc + condyle forward during mouth opening. TMJ dislocation: condyle slips anterior to articular eminence → jaw locked open; reposition by downward + backward pressure on mandible.

</details>

<!-- SOURCE: Anatomy_NEET_PG_PYT20182023_1_1.pdf 2023 | OPTIONS_GENERATED: false -->

### Q80 — Glomerulus — histology

Histological image shows rounded tuft of anastomosing capillaries, fenestrated endothelium, mesangium, Bowman's capsule. Structure?

- A. Hassall's corpuscle
- B. Pancreatic islet cells
- C. Spleen red pulp
- D. Glomerulus

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**D. Glomerulus** — Glomerulus: tuft of fenestrated capillaries + mesangium (cells + matrix); filtering membrane = fenestrated endothelium + GBM + podocytes (foot processes). Afferent arteriole in, efferent out. Podocyte injury → proteinuria. Mesangial proliferation → IgA nephropathy.

</details>

<!-- SOURCE: Anatomy_NEET_PG_PYT20182023_1_1.pdf 2023 | OPTIONS_GENERATED: false -->

### Q81 — Fibula neck injury — consequence

Injury at the neck of the fibula (marked site 3) causes failure of:

- A. Dorsiflexion (foot drop)
- B. Plantarflexion
- C. Inversion
- D. Knee flexion

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A. Dorsiflexion (foot drop)** — Common peroneal nerve winds around fibular neck → foot drop (loss of dorsiflexion by tibialis anterior, EHL, EDL) + eversion loss (peroneus longus + brevis) + sensory loss dorsum foot. Tibial nerve injury → plantarflexion + inversion loss.

</details>

<!-- SOURCE: Anatomy_NEET_PG_PYT20182023_1_1.pdf 2023 | OPTIONS_GENERATED: false -->

### Q82 — Patent ductus arteriosus — aortic arch

Patent ductus arteriosus is due to a defect in which aortic arch?

- A. 4th right
- B. 4th left
- C. 6th right
- D. 6th left

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**D. 6th left** — Left 6th arch → proximal left pulmonary artery + ductus arteriosus. PDA = failure of ductus to close. Treated with indomethacin (NSAIDs close PDA) in premature; prostaglandins keep PDA open in duct-dependent congenital heart disease. Right 6th arch → right pulmonary artery only (no ductus right).

</details>

<!-- SOURCE: Anatomy_NEET_PG_PYT20182023_1_1.pdf 2023 | OPTIONS_GENERATED: false -->

### Q83 — Allantois derivative

Structure A (allantois) gives rise to which of the following?

- A. Medial umbilical ligament
- B. Median umbilical ligament
- C. Meckel's diverticulum
- D. Ligamentum teres

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B. Median umbilical ligament** — Allantois → distal portion = urachus → obliterates = median umbilical ligament (single, midline). Medial umbilical ligaments (paired) = obliterated umbilical arteries. Ligamentum teres = left umbilical vein. Meckel's = vitello-intestinal duct remnant (2% population, 2 feet from ileocecal valve, 2 inches long).

</details>

<!-- SOURCE: Anatomy_NEET_PG_PYT20182023_1_1.pdf 2023 | OPTIONS_GENERATED: false -->

### Q84 — Portal hypertension — portosystemic anastomosis

Patient with portal hypertension: anastomosis between which veins?

- A. Left colic and middle colic veins
- B. Esophageal veins and left gastric vein
- C. Superior rectal and phrenic veins
- D. Sigmoid and superior rectal veins

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B. Esophageal veins and left gastric vein** — Porto-systemic shunts in portal hypertension: (1) left gastric ↔ esophageal veins = esophageal varices (most dangerous); (2) superior rectal ↔ inferior/middle rectal = rectal varices; (3) paraumbilical ↔ epigastric = caput medusae; (4) retroperitoneal (Retzius) veins.

</details>

<!-- SOURCE: Anatomy_NEET_PG_PYT20182023_1_1.pdf 2023 | OPTIONS_GENERATED: false -->

### Q85 — Parotid pain — nerve responsible

Patient with acute parotid swelling complains of pain. Most likely cause of pain?

- A. Facial nerve
- B. Greater auricular nerve
- C. Auriculotemporal nerve
- D. Investing layer of cervical fascia

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B. Greater auricular nerve** — Greater auricular nerve (C2, C3) provides sensory supply to parotid gland capsule. Acute sialadenitis → capsule stretching → pain via greater auricular nerve. Facial nerve is motor within parotid (no sensory to gland). Auriculotemporal nerve → TMJ + anterior auricle + temporal skin.

</details>

<!-- SOURCE: Anatomy_NEET_PG_PYT20182023_1_1.pdf 2023 | OPTIONS_GENERATED: false -->

### Q86 — Common hepatic artery ligation — vessels affected

Ligation of the common hepatic artery impairs blood supply through:

- A. Right gastric and right gastroepiploic arteries
- B. Right gastric and left gastric arteries
- C. Right gastroepiploic and short gastric arteries
- D. Right gastric and short gastric arteries

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A. Right gastric and right gastroepiploic arteries** — CHA → proper hepatic (→ right gastric + right/left hepatic) + gastroduodenal (→ right gastroepiploic + superior pancreaticoduodenal). Ligation of CHA: right gastric + right gastroepiploic lose their primary supply. Left gastric = celiac trunk direct branch (unaffected).

</details>

<!-- SOURCE: Anatomy_NEET_PG_PYT20182023_1_1.pdf 2020 | OPTIONS_GENERATED: false -->

### Q87 — Dorsal scapular nerve — muscle supplied

Image shows rhomboid muscles. Nerve supply?

- A. Dorsal scapular nerve
- B. Suprascapular nerve
- C. Dorsal rami of C1
- D. Subscapular nerve

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A. Dorsal scapular nerve** — Dorsal scapular nerve (C5, from roots) supplies: rhomboid major, rhomboid minor, levator scapulae. Suprascapular = supraspinatus + infraspinatus (+ sensation shoulder). Long thoracic (C5, C6, C7) = serratus anterior. Accessory (XI) = trapezius.

</details>

<!-- SOURCE: Anatomy_NEET_PG_PYT20182023_1_1.pdf 2020 | OPTIONS_GENERATED: false -->

### Q88 — Popeye sign — biceps tendon

Image shows muscle belly bulging on elbow flexion. Sign identified?

- A. Popeye sign
- B. Hill-Sachs sign
- C. Rising sun sign
- D. Sulcus sign

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A. Popeye sign** — Rupture of long head of biceps tendon → proximal migration of muscle belly → bulging "Popeye" deformity on elbow flexion. Long head (within bicipital groove) more commonly ruptured than short head. Conservative in elderly; surgical repair in young/athletes/manual workers.

</details>

<!-- SOURCE: Anatomy_NEET_PG_PYT20182023_1_1.pdf 2020 | OPTIONS_GENERATED: false -->

### Q89 — Costotransverse joint type

The costotransverse joint (costal tubercle to transverse process) is a:

- A. Primary cartilaginous (synchondrosis)
- B. Secondary cartilaginous (symphysis)
- C. Synovial joint
- D. Fibrous joint

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**C. Synovial joint** — Costotransverse joint = synovial plane joint. Thoracic wall joint types: synovial = costovertebral, costotransverse, interchondral (7th–10th); synchondrosis = costochondral joints; symphysis = manubriosternal + xiphisternal. All synovial joints allow movement during respiration.

</details>

<!-- SOURCE: Anatomy_NEET_PG_PYT20182023_1_1.pdf 2020 | OPTIONS_GENERATED: false -->

### Q90 — Lumbrical nerve supply

Nerve supply of the lateral two lumbrical muscles:

- A. Posterior interosseus nerve
- B. Median nerve
- C. Anterior interosseus nerve
- D. Ulnar nerve

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B. Median nerve** — LOAF muscles (all median nerve): Lumbricals 1+2 (lateral two), Opponens pollicis, Abductor pollicis brevis, Flexor pollicis brevis (superficial head). Medial two lumbricals + all interossei + hypothenar (except FPB) = ulnar nerve (deep branch).

</details>

<!-- SOURCE: Anatomy_NEET_PG_PYT20182023_1_1.pdf 2019 | OPTIONS_GENERATED: false -->

### Q91 — Projection fibers — cerebral hemisphere

The internal capsule is an example of which type of white matter fiber?

- A. Short association fibers
- B. Long association fibers
- C. Projection fibers
- D. Commissural fibers

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**C. Projection fibers** — Internal capsule = projection fibers (bidirectional: cortex ↔ subcortical/brainstem/spinal cord). Anterior limb: frontopontine + thalamocortical; posterior limb: corticospinal + corticobulbar + thalamocortical. Association = same hemisphere. Commissural = between hemispheres (corpus callosum).

</details>

<!-- SOURCE: Anatomy_NEET_PG_PYT20182023_1_1.pdf 2019 | OPTIONS_GENERATED: false -->

### Q92 — Great auricular nerve — thickening in leprosy

Most commonly enlarged peripheral nerve in leprosy?

- A. Great auricular nerve
- B. Lesser occipital nerve
- C. Transverse cervical nerve
- D. Supraclavicular nerve

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A. Great auricular nerve** — Great auricular nerve (C2, C3) at Erb's point (posterior SCM): most commonly palpable thickened nerve in leprosy. Other commonly affected peripheral nerves in leprosy: ulnar (claw hand), common peroneal (foot drop), posterior tibial (plantar anesthesia), facial (lagophthalmos), superficial radial.

</details>

<!-- SOURCE: Anatomy_NEET_PG_PYT20182023_1_1.pdf 2018 | OPTIONS_GENERATED: false -->

### Q93 — Trigeminal neuralgia — nerve involved

Unilateral throbbing headache + hemifacial pain on clenching + photophobia + lacrimation, normal pupils. Nerve involved?

- A. Facial nerve (VII)
- B. Trigeminal nerve (V)
- C. Oculomotor nerve (III)
- D. Abducens nerve (VI)

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B. Trigeminal nerve (V)** — Hemifacial pain on jaw clenching + sensory features = trigeminal neuralgia (CN V). CN III lesion → pupillary dilation (blown pupil). Cluster headache = trigeminal-autonomic cephalalgia (involves V + autonomic). Normal pupillary reflex rules out CN III/optic nerve involvement.

</details>

<!-- SOURCE: Anatomy_NEET_PG_PYT20182023_1_1.pdf 2022 | OPTIONS_GENERATED: false -->

### Q94 — Lingual nerve — relationship to Wharton's duct

The lingual nerve is at risk during submandibular gland surgery because:

- A. It passes through the submandibular gland
- B. It winds around (below then above) the submandibular duct
- C. It is attached to the submandibular fascia
- D. It supplies motor fibers to the mylohyoid

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B. It winds around the submandibular duct** — Lingual nerve (V3) passes lateral → inferior → medial to Wharton's duct ("lingual nerve takes a curve around the duct of Wharton"). Injury → ipsilateral loss of taste + somatic sensation of anterior 2/3 tongue. The nerve to mylohyoid (branch of IAN) supplies mylohyoid + anterior belly digastric.

</details>

<!-- SOURCE: Anatomy_NEET_PG_PYT20182023_1_1.pdf 2021 | OPTIONS_GENERATED: false -->

### Q95 — Gag reflex — cranial nerves

The gag reflex: afferent limb = ?, efferent limb = ?

- A. X (afferent) and IX (efferent)
- B. IX (afferent) and X (efferent)
- C. V (afferent) and XII (efferent)
- D. IX (afferent) and XII (efferent)

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B. IX (afferent) and X (efferent)** — Gag reflex: afferent = glossopharyngeal (CN IX, from posterior pharyngeal wall/soft palate); efferent = vagus (CN X, via pharyngeal plexus → constrictors). Uvula deviation toward normal side on phonation = X palsy on opposite side. "Say 'Ahh'" tests CN X motor.

</details>

<!-- SOURCE: Anatomy_NEET_PG_PYT20182023_1_1.pdf 2021 | OPTIONS_GENERATED: false -->

### Q96 — Fibula neck — common peroneal nerve vulnerability

Why is the common peroneal nerve particularly vulnerable at the fibular neck?

- A. It is tightly enclosed in a fascial compartment
- B. It has a superficial course winding around the bone with no overlying muscle
- C. It passes through an osteoligamentous tunnel
- D. It is the thickest peripheral nerve in the leg

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B. Superficial course winding around bone with no overlying muscle** — Common peroneal nerve winds around fibular neck just deep to the fascia, with no muscular protection → susceptible to compression (plaster casts, prolonged squatting, knee dislocation). Most common nerve injured in lower limb trauma. Foot drop is the result.

</details>

<!-- SOURCE: Anatomy_NEET_PG_PYT20182023_1_1.pdf 2023 | OPTIONS_GENERATED: false -->

### Q97 — Median umbilical ligament vs medial umbilical ligament

Which structure represents the obliterated urachus (allantois remnant)?

- A. Medial umbilical ligament (paired)
- B. Median umbilical ligament (single, midline)
- C. Ligamentum teres hepatis
- D. Ligamentum venosum

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B. Median umbilical ligament** — Mnemonic: Median (midline, single) = urachus (allantois); Medial (bilateral pair) = obliterated umbilical arteries; Ligamentum teres = left umbilical vein; Ligamentum venosum = ductus venosus. Urachal anomalies: patent urachus (urine from umbilicus), urachal cyst, urachal sinus.

</details>

<!-- SOURCE: Anatomy_NEET_PG_PYT20182023_1_1.pdf 2023 | OPTIONS_GENERATED: false -->

### Q98 — Esophageal varices — portosystemic level

Where does the clinically significant portosystemic anastomosis for esophageal varices occur?

- A. Lower rectum
- B. Lower esophagus/gastric cardia
- C. Periumbilical region
- D. Retroperitoneum

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B. Lower esophagus/gastric cardia** — Left gastric vein (portal) ↔ esophageal veins (azygos/systemic): esophageal varices at gastroesophageal junction. Most dangerous portosystemic shunt (high risk of rupture → torrential hemorrhage, mortality 20–30% per bleed). TIPS procedure creates intrahepatic shunt to decompress.

</details>

<!-- SOURCE: Anatomy_NEET_PG_PYT20182023_1_1.pdf 2023 | OPTIONS_GENERATED: false -->

## Year 2015

### Q99 — External carotid artery: NOT a branch

All of the following are branches of the external carotid artery **except**:

- A. Superior thyroid artery
- B. Anterior ethmoidal artery
- C. Occipital artery
- D. Posterior auricular artery

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B. Anterior ethmoidal artery** — A branch of the **ophthalmic artery** (from internal carotid), not the ECA. ECA branches (mnemonic: Some Anatomists Like Freaking Out Poor Medical Students): Superior thyroid, Ascending pharyngeal, Lingual, Facial, Occipital, Posterior auricular, Maxillary, Superficial temporal.

</details>

### Q100 — Cranial nerve palsy localisation (III, IV, VI + V1)

Paralysis of 3rd, 4th, 6th nerves with involvement of the ophthalmic division of the 5th nerve localises the lesion to:

- A. Cavernous sinus
- B. Apex of orbit
- C. Brainstem
- D. Base of skull

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A. Cavernous sinus** — The cavernous sinus contains CN III, IV, V1, V2, and VI. Apex of orbit syndrome (Rochon-Duvigneaud) involves III, IV, V1, VI and the optic nerve (II). Brainstem lesions usually cause crossed signs (ipsilateral cranial nerves + contralateral long-tract signs).

</details>

<!-- SOURCE: medicallegends.blogspot.com AIPGMEE 2015 Anatomy | OPTIONS_GENERATED: false -->

---
### Q101 — Carpal tunnel contents: NOT included

The carpal tunnel contains all of the following **except**:

- A. Median nerve
- B. Flexor pollicis longus tendon
- C. Flexor carpi radialis tendon
- D. Flexor digitorum superficialis tendons

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**C. Flexor carpi radialis tendon** — FCR passes **outside** the carpal tunnel, in its own osteofibrous sheath within the flexor retinaculum. Carpal tunnel contains: median nerve + 9 tendons (FPL, 4× FDS, 4× FDP).

</details>

<!-- SOURCE: medicallegends.blogspot.com AIPGMEE 2015 Anatomy | OPTIONS_GENERATED: false -->

### Q102 — Femoral ring boundaries: NOT a boundary

The femoral ring is bounded by the following structures **except**:

- A. Femoral vein
- B. Inguinal ligament
- C. Femoral artery
- D. Lacunar ligament

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**C. Femoral artery** — Femoral ring boundaries: Anterior = inguinal ligament; Posterior = pectineal ligament/superior ramus of pubis; Medial = lacunar ligament; Lateral = femoral vein. The femoral **artery** lies lateral to the femoral vein, outside the femoral ring.

</details>

<!-- SOURCE: medicallegends.blogspot.com AIPGMEE 2015 Anatomy | OPTIONS_GENERATED: false -->

---
### Q103 — Vas deferens: false statement

All of the following statements regarding vas deferens are true **except**:

- A. Terminal part is dilated to form ampulla
- B. It crosses the ureter in the region of the ischial spine
- C. It passes lateral to the inferior epigastric artery at the deep inguinal ring
- D. It is separated from the base of bladder by the peritoneum

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**D. Separated from base of bladder by peritoneum** — FALSE. The vas deferens is in **direct contact** with the base of the bladder (no peritoneum between them). The terminal part forms the ampulla, it crosses the ureter near the ischial spine ("water under the bridge"), and passes lateral to the inferior epigastric artery at the deep inguinal ring.

</details>

<!-- SOURCE: medicallegends.blogspot.com AIPGMEE 2015 Anatomy | OPTIONS_GENERATED: false -->

---
### Q104 — Chorda tympani nerve: false statement

The following statements concerning the chorda tympani nerve are true **except** that it:

- A. Carries secretomotor fibres to the submandibular gland
- B. Joins the lingual nerve in the infratemporal fossa
- C. Is a branch of the facial nerve
- D. Contains postganglionic parasympathetic fibres

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**D. Contains postganglionic parasympathetic fibres** — FALSE. Chorda tympani carries **preganglionic** parasympathetic fibres (from superior salivatory nucleus) that synapse in the submandibular ganglion; postganglionic fibres then innervate the submandibular and sublingual glands. It also carries taste from anterior 2/3 of tongue.

</details>

<!-- SOURCE: medicallegends.blogspot.com AIPGMEE 2015 Anatomy | OPTIONS_GENERATED: false -->

---
### Q105 — Sacrococcygeal joint type

The type of joint between the sacrum and the coccyx is a:

- A. Symphysis
- B. Synostosis
- C. Synchondrosis
- D. Syndesmosis

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A. Symphysis** — The sacrococcygeal joint is a secondary cartilaginous joint (symphysis) with a fibrocartilaginous disc between the apex of the sacrum and the base of the coccyx. May ossify (synostosis) in old age.

</details>

<!-- SOURCE: medicallegends.blogspot.com AIPGMEE 2015 Anatomy | OPTIONS_GENERATED: false -->

---
### Q106 — Prostatic urethra: false feature

The prostatic urethra is characterised by all of the following **except** that it:

- A. Is the widest and most dilatable part of the urethra
- B. Presents a concavity posteriorly
- C. Lies closer to the anterior surface of the prostate
- D. Receives prostatic ductules along its posterior wall

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B. Presents a concavity posteriorly** — FALSE. The prostatic urethra presents a concavity **anteriorly** (urethral crest projects on the posterior wall). It is the widest and most dilatable part, lies closer to the anterior surface, and prostatic ductules open on the posterior wall (in the prostatic sinuses lateral to the urethral crest).

</details>

<!-- SOURCE: medicallegends.blogspot.com AIPGMEE 2015 Anatomy | OPTIONS_GENERATED: false -->

---
### Q107 — Uterine lymphatic drainage: NOT a destination

The following group of lymph nodes receives lymphatics from the uterus **except**:

- A. External iliac
- B. Internal iliac
- C. Superficial inguinal
- D. Deep inguinal

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**D. Deep inguinal** — Lymphatic drainage of uterus: fundus → para-aortic (along ovarian vessels); body → external iliac; cervix → internal iliac, external iliac, sacral; round ligament → **superficial** inguinal. Deep inguinal nodes are NOT a drainage site for the uterus.

</details>

<!-- SOURCE: medicallegends.blogspot.com AIPGMEE 2015 Anatomy | OPTIONS_GENERATED: false -->

---
### Q108 — Epiphyseal plate growth processes: NOT a feature

All of the following physiological processes occur during growth at the epiphyseal plate **except**:

- A. Proliferation and hypertrophy
- B. Calcification and ossification
- C. Vasculogenesis and erosion
- D. Replacement of red bone marrow with yellow bone marrow

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**D. Replacement of red bone marrow with yellow bone marrow** — FALSE. Red-to-yellow marrow conversion is a separate developmental process occurring in the medullary cavity (begins in distal long bones in infancy, progresses centrally). At the epiphyseal plate, chondrocytes undergo: resting → proliferation → hypertrophy → calcification → ossification, with vascular invasion (vasculogenesis) and matrix erosion.

</details>

<!-- SOURCE: medicallegends.blogspot.com AIPGMEE 2015 Anatomy | OPTIONS_GENERATED: false -->

---
### Q109 — Benign prostatic hypertrophy: lobe enlarged

Benign prostatic hypertrophy results in obstruction of the urinary tract. The condition is associated with enlargement of the:

- A. Entire prostate gland
- B. Lateral lobes
- C. Median lobe
- D. Posterior lobes

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**C. Median lobe** — Median lobe enlargement (transition zone) causes obstruction of the internal urethral orifice → BPH symptoms. Posterior lobe is the typical site for prostate **cancer** (peripheral zone). Lateral lobes also enlarge but predominantly the median lobe causes obstruction.

</details>

<!-- SOURCE: medicallegends.blogspot.com AIPGMEE 2015 Anatomy | OPTIONS_GENERATED: false -->

---
### Q110 — Per rectal examination: NOT palpable anteriorly

In an adult male, on per rectal examination, the following structures can be felt anteriorly **except**:

- A. Internal iliac lymph nodes
- B. Bulb of the penis
- C. Prostate
- D. Seminal vesicle (when enlarged)

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A. Internal iliac lymph nodes** — Internal iliac nodes lie **deep in the pelvis along the iliac vessels** and are NOT palpable on PR examination. Anteriorly palpable on PR: prostate (normal/enlarged), bulb of penis, enlarged seminal vesicles, bladder base, sigmoid colon.

</details>

<!-- SOURCE: medicallegends.blogspot.com AIPGMEE 2015 Anatomy | OPTIONS_GENERATED: false -->

### Q111 — Upper limb deformity in Erbs palsy

Upper limb deformity in Erb's palsy?

- A. Adduction and lateral rotation of arm
- B. Adduction and medial rotation of arm
- C. Abduction and lateral rotation of arm
- D. Abduction and medial rotation of arm

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B. Adduction and medial rotation of arm** — C5-C6 injury paralyzes deltoid, supraspinatus, infraspinatus, biceps; unopposed pectoralis and subscapularis cause adduction and medial rotation (waiter's tip).

</details>

---

---
### Q112 — Nerve roots involved in Erbs palsy

Nerve roots involved in Erb's palsy:

- A. C5, C6
- B. C6, C7
- C. C7,C8,T1
- D. C5,C6,C7,C8,T1

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A. C5, C6** — Erb's palsy involves upper trunk of brachial plexus (C5-C6) from excessive head-shoulder traction; Klumpke's involves lower trunk C8-T1.

</details>

---

### Q113 — Superior to inferior -vein,

Superior to inferior -vein,

- A. Right :- (i) Eparterial bronchus (superior most)' (ii) Pulmonary
- B. Left :- (i) Puhnonary artery (superior most), (ii) Primary/Principal
- C. Obturator
- D. Paraaortic

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**D. Paraaortic** — **D. Paraaortic** — Para-aortic lymph nodes are the final common drainage pathway for gonadal, renal, and pelvic lymphatics, draining superiorly to the cisterna chyli; relevant in testicular and ovarian cancer staging.

</details>

### Q114 — Root value of thoracodorsal nerve

Root value of thoracodorsal nerve ?

- A. C5,C6,C7
- B. C8,T1
- C. C6,C7,C8
- D. Ti T2

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**C. C6,C7,C8** — Thoracodorsal nerve (C6,C7,C8) arises from posterior cord of brachial plexus and supplies latissimus dorsi muscle.

</details>

### Q115 — Teres minor is supplied by

Teres minor is supplied by

- A. Suprascapular nerve
- B. Infrascapular nerve
- C. Thoracodorsal nerve
- D. Axillary nerve

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**D. Axillary nerve** — Axillary nerve (C5,C6) from posterior cord supplies teres minor and deltoid; also gives upper lateral cutaneous nerve of arm.

</details>

### Q116 — Limb shortening

Limb shortening

- A. 1,2 true & 3,4 false
- B. 2,3 True & 1,4 false
- C. 1,2,3 true & 4 false
- D. All are true

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**D. All are true** — Limb shortening can be true (bone loss), apparent (pelvic tilt/adduction), supratrochanteric (above greater trochanter), and infratrochanteric (below) - all causes are valid.

</details>

### Q117 — Insertion of levator scapulae is

Insertion of levator scapulae is ?

- A. Lateral border of scapula
- B. Suprolateral part of scapula
- C. Superior part of medial scapula border
- D. Inferior angle of scapula

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**C. Superior part of medial scapula border** — Levator scapulae inserts on medial border of scapula above the root of the spine; elevates and rotates scapula downward.

</details>

### Q118 — Number of lobes in breast

Number of lobes in breast

- A. 5
- B. 10
- C. 15
- D. 30

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**C. 15** — Breast contains 15-20 lobes arranged radially, each drained by a lactiferous duct opening separately at the nipple.

### Q119 — Which of the following arises from

Which of the following arises from infraglenoid tubercle -

- A. Longheadofbiceps
- B. Longheadoftriceps
- C. Shortheadofbiceps
- D. Coracobrachialis

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B. Longheadoftriceps** — Long head of triceps arises from infraglenoid tubercle of scapula; long head of biceps from supraglenoid tubercle.

</details>

### Q120 — Not attached on medial border of

Not attached on medial border of scapula ?

- A. Serratus anterior
- B. Levator scapulae
- C. Rhamboides major
- D. Teres major

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**D. Teres major** — Teres major attaches to lateral border (lower part) of scapula; serratus anterior, levator scapulae, rhomboids attach to medial border.

### Q121 — Medial boundry of Cubital fossa

Medial boundry of Cubital fossa ?

- A. Brachioradialis
- B. Pronator teres
- C. Supinator
- D. None

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B. Pronator teres** — Cubital fossa medial boundary is pronator teres; lateral is brachioradialis; floor is brachialis and supinator; roof is bicipital aponeurosis.

### Q122 — True about lumbricals is

True about lumbricals is

- A. Flex IP joints and extends MCP joint
- B. 1st and 2nd supplied by radial nerve
- C. 3 and 4 supplied by superficial branch of ulnar
- D. Origin from tendons of flexor digitorum profundus

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**D. Origin from tendons of flexor digitorum profundus** — Lumbricals originate from flexor digitorum profundus tendons, insert on extensor expansion, flex MCP and extend IP joints.

</details>

### Q123 — Lateral boundary of cubital fossa is

Lateral boundary of cubital fossa is formed by:

- A. Brachioradialis
- B. Pronator teres

- D. Biceps

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A. Brachioradialis** — Brachioradialis (radial nerve, C5-C6) forms lateral boundary of cubital fossa; pronator teres forms medial boundary.

### Q124 — Muscle of Arm with additional supinator

Muscle of Arm with additional supinator action?

- A. Brachialis
- B. Biceps
- C. Coracobrachialis
- D. Triceps

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B. Biceps** — Biceps brachii is the most powerful supinator of forearm (when elbow flexed), in addition to flexing elbow.

### Q125 — The nerve supply of nail bed

The nerve supply of nail bed of index finger is ?

- A. Superficial br of radial nerve
- B. Deep br of radial nerve

- D. Ulnar nerve

### Q126 — Which leaves the pelvis

Which leaves the pelvis ?

- A. Piriformis
- B. Sciatic nerve
- C. Superior gluteal vessel
- D. Inferior gluteal vessel

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

### Q127 — Abduction and adduction of foot occurs

Abduction and adduction of foot occurs at which joints

- A. Ankle
- B. Subtalar

### Q128 — All are true about femoral triangle,

All are true about femoral triangle, EXCEPT?

- A. Lateral margin is formed by sartorius
- B. Floor is formed by adductor longus
- C. Contains the femoral vessels
- D. None of the above

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

### Q129 — Content of femoral canal

Content of femoral canal

- A. Femoral branch of genitofemoral nerve
- B. Genital branch of genitofemoral nerve
- C. Femoral vein
- D. Lymph node

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**D. Lymph node** — Femoral canal contains lymph node of Cloquet, lymphatics, and loose areolar tissue; allows venous distension and is the site of femoral hernia.

</details>

### Q130 — A patient presents with defective adduction

A patient presents with defective adduction of the hip joint and pains in the hip and knee joint. Which nerve is involved

- A. Obturator nerve
- B. Femoral nerve
- C. Saphenous nerve
- D. Sciatic nerve

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**A. Obturator nerve** — Obturator nerve (L2-L4) supplies adductors of thigh; gives articular branches to hip and knee joints (Hilton's law - referred pain).

</details>

### Q131 — Structure which lies outside the femoral

Structure which lies outside the femoral sheath

- A. Femoral artery
- B. Femoral nerve
- C. Femoral vein
- D. Genitofemoral nerve

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**B. Femoral nerve** — Femoral nerve lies lateral to and outside the femoral sheath; sheath contains femoral artery, vein, and canal (lymphatics).

</details>

### Q132 — Which structure(s) passes behind the inguinal

Which structure(s) passes behind the inguinal ligament:

- A. Femoral branch of genitofemoral nerve
- B. Femoral vein
- C. Psoas major
- D. All

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**D. All** — Behind inguinal ligament: psoas major, iliacus, femoral nerve/artery/vein, lymphatics, femoral branch of genitofemoral nerve, lateral cutaneous nerve of thigh.

</details>

### Q133 — Lateral border of ischeorectal fossa is

Lateral border of ischeorectal fossa is formed by?

- A. Gluteus maximus
- B. Perineal membrane
- C. Pelvic diaphram
- D. Obturator internus

<details><summary>Answer</summary>

**D. Obturator internus** — Ischiorectal fossa lateral wall: obturator internus muscle with its fascia containing pudendal canal (Alcock's); medial wall is levator ani.

</details>
